Chapter 1

Points to Remember

Over 1,100 rapid revision one-liners and high-yield Q&As across National, International, Economy, Sci-Tech, UP Special, and Sports.

National Points to Remember (राष्ट्रीय स्मरणीय तथ्य)

  • Where was the National Urban Conclave 2026 held? (October 25, 2026) - Bhubaneswar
  • What was the main focus of the Conclave? - Strengthening urban governance, planning and service delivery towards Viksit Bharat 2047
  • Where was RBI’s new Data Centre and Cybersecurity Training Institute inaugurated? (October 25, 2026) - Bhubaneswar, Odisha
  • How many Farmer IDs had been created by October 23, 2026? - 11 crore
  • Under which mission is the Farmer ID initiative implemented? - Digital Agriculture Mission
  • What was the deadline for creating 11 crore Farmer IDs? - March 31, 2027
  • What is the main purpose of a Farmer ID? - To provide a unique digital identity for easier access to government schemes, bank credit, crop insurance and disaster relief.
  • Which strategic buyer was approved to acquire a 60.72 per cent stake in IDBI Bank? (October 14, 2026)
  • Approximately ₹52,878.08 crore.
  • Who inaugurated the tenth edition of India Mobile Congress, 2026? (8 October 2026)
  • Which rabi crop received the highest absolute increase in its Minimum Support Price?
  • Who inaugurated Arogya Manthan, 2026? (25 September 2026) – Union Minister for Health and Family Welfare Jagat Prakash Nadda.
  • Who inaugurated the 昀椀fth edition of Semicon India, 2026? (17 September 2026)
  • Under which country’s chairmanship was the 18th BRICS Summit held? (12–13 September 2026)
  • In which city was the 18th BRICS Summit held?
  • Who inaugurated the Department of Military Medicine? – Defence Minister Rajnath Singh.
  • Which constitutional amendment granted constitutional status to the Panchayati Raj system?
  • The 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act, 1992.

Its institutional journey began on 1 October 1926 , when India’s 昀椀rst Public Service Commission was established.
  • At that time, it was an advisory body whose Chairman and members were appointed by the Secretary of State for India in London.
  • Various programmes and activities were organised throughout the year to mark the UPSC’s centenary. The year-long celebrations began on 1 October 2025 and continued until 1 October 2026.  Key Facts
  • The UPSC organised a two: day centenary event titled ‘Manthan’ at Bharat Mandapam, New Delhi, from 30 September to 1 October 2026.  The Journey
  • The Indian examination centre was shifted to Delhi in 1928.  Major Milestones Year Major milestone 1937 On 1 April, the Commission was renamed the Federal Public Service Commission. 1938 Combined competitive examinations were introduced for several services, marking the beginning of integrated recruitment to multiple services. 1947 Shri H. K. Kripalani became the first Indian Chairman following independence; the first examination was held in December and the IAS replaced the ICS. 1950 On 26 January , the Federal Public Service Commission was renamed the Union Public Service Commission. Shri R.N. Banerjee assumed charge on January 14, 1949, and continued as Chairman following the Institution’s Constitutional transition on January 26, 1950. 1951 The All India Services Act was enacted. 1979 The three-stage Civil Services Examination— Preliminary Examination, Main Examination and Interview—was introduced. 2011 The Civil Services Aptitude Test (CSAT) was introduced.   • Class: 1 heirs will include the spouse, children, and parents of the deceased.  Live-In Relationship Provisions
  • The Act makes registration of live: in relationships mandatory within one month, including for non- residents.
  • Failure to register a live: in relationship within one month may attract imprisonment of up to three months or a fine of up to `10,000 (and continued failure may attract six months of imprisonment or `25,000 or both).
  • Any child born out of a live: in relationship will be considered fully legitimate.  Penalties Under the Act
  • Marriage within prohibited relationships, unless protected by valid customs, shall be punishable with imprisonment of up to six months and a fine of up to `50,000.
  • Non: registration of marriage or divorce within the prescribed period shall attract a penalty of `10,000 (and `25,000 after first notice).
  • Forged documents in registration matters may lead to imprisonment of up to three months, or a fine of up to `25,000, or both.  Gujarat Uniform Civil Code 2026 President Droupadi Murmu assented to the Gujarat Uniform Civil Code Bill on 17 September 2026. The Public Examinations Prevention of Unfair Means Amendment Act 2026 The Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Amendment Act, 2026, amends the 2024 Act, which came into force on June 21, 2024.
  • Dr. Jitendra Singh introduced the Bill in the Lok Sabha on July 27, 2026. It passed the Lok Sabha on July 29 and the Rajya Sabha on July 30, and received presidential assent on July 31, 2026.  Public examination authorities covered
  • The Act covers examinations conducted by the Union Public Service Commission, Staff Selection Commission, Railway Recruitment Boards, Institute of Banking Personnel Selection, National Testing Agency, Central Government ministries and attached of昀椀ces, and other authorities noti昀椀ed by the Central Government.  Major provisions  Enhanced penalties
  • For individuals using unfair means, imprisonment increases from 3–5 to 5–10 years, and the maximum 昀椀ne increase from ₹10 lakh to ₹50 lakh. For service providers, the maximum 昀椀ne rises from ₹1 crore to ₹5 crore and debarment expands from up to four years to eight years. Proportionate examination costs shall also be recovered.
  • Directors, senior managers or persons in charge who consent to or connive in an offence face 5–10 years’ imprisonment and a ₹5 crore 昀椀ne, compared with 3–10 years of imprisonment and a 昀椀ne of ₹1 crore earlier. Organised crime attracts 7–10 years’ imprisonment and a minimum ₹10 crore 昀椀ne, compared with 5–10 years of imprisonment and a 昀椀ne of a minimum ₹1 crore 昀椀ne under the 2024 Act.  Investigation
  • The amendment introduces a two: month investigation period, counted from the date of recording information for police investigations, referral for central agencies, and the date of the noti昀椀cation constituting a Special Task Force (STF).  Special Fast Track Courts
  • Every state and Union Territory must designate one or more Courts of Session as Special Fast- Track Courts in consultation with Chief Justice of the relevant High Court. Trials must be completed within three months of 昀椀ling the charge sheet, or within three months of transfer in pending cases. State governments and UT administrations must appoint Special Public Prosecutors under the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita (BNSS), 2023.  Appeals

India and It’s Neighbours; Other & Related Developments

  • India and It’s Neighbours; Other Asian Countries India–Switzerland President Guy Parmelin’s State Visit to India Swiss Confederation President Guy Parmelin visited India from October 5–7, 2026 , marking the first anniversary of the India–European Free Trade Association (EFTA) Trade and Economic Partnership Agreement (TEPA) . On 5 October, he paid tribute to Mahatma Gandhi at Rajghat, New Delhi, held bilateral talks with Prime Minister Narendra Modi at Hyderabad House , and met President Droupadi Murmu at Rashtrapati Bhavan . Both countries emphasised cooperation in the 3Ts—Trade, Technology and Talent, alongside developing resilient global supply chains.
  • India: EFTA Agreement: Established in 1960, EFTA comprises Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway and Switzerland. Switzerland is not a member of the European Economic Area (EEA).
  • India: EFTA TEPA was signed on 10 March 2024 and entered into force on 1 October 2025. It aims to facilitate US$100 billion in investments and one million direct jobs in India over 15 years.
  • Five Major Agreements and Arrangements: 1. Migration and Mobility Partnership Memorandum of Understanding (MoU): Promotes legal migration and professional mobility, including multiple-entry visas valid for up to 昀椀ve years. 2. Young Professionals Exchange Agreement: Provides for the exchange of 300 professionals annually from each country , expandable to 500, through temporary employment. 3. Transport Technology MoU: Signed between India’s Ministry of Road Transport and Highways and Switzerland’s Federal Department of Economic Affairs, Education and Research A. International Relations (EAER) to promote sustainable transport, electric vehicles, ropeways and tunnel technologies. 4. Research Letter of Intent (LoI): Signed between India’s Department of Science and Technology (DST) and the Swiss National Science Foundation (SNSF) to strengthen long-term scienti昀椀c research. 5. Innovation Implementation Arrangement: Signed between DST and the Zurich University of Applied Sciences (ZHAW) to promote technology transfer, entrepreneurship and the Indo- Swiss Academia–Industry Training (AIT India) programme for early-stage startups.
  • The inaugural Indo: Swiss Research Flagship Event was held in New Delhi on 6 October 2026, attended by President Parmelin and Union Minister Dr. Jitendra Singh.
  • The Indo: Swiss Research Framework (ISRF) aims to strengthen scienti昀椀c networks, knowledge exchange and academia–industry collaboration. Bilateral scienti昀椀c cooperation began in 2003, followed by the Indo-Swiss Joint Research Programme in 2005.
  • The Second India: EFTA Prosperity Summit was held at Bharat Mandapam, New Delhi, on 7 October 2026 , reviewing TEPA implementation and investment opportunities.
  • India: Switzerland relations are rooted in the Treaty of Friendship signed on 14 August 1948. After 14 negotiation rounds, CETA & Related Developments

  • After 14 negotiation rounds, CETA was concluded on 6 May 2025 and signed in London on 24 July 2025. DCC was signed on 10 February 2026.
  • CETA is a next: generation free trade agreement with 30 chapters covering goods, services, investment, digital trade, intellectual property, government procurement, competition, innovation, sustainability and MSMEs.
  • The UK provides zero: duty access to nearly 99 per cent of Indian tariff lines, covering almost the entire value of Indian exports to the UK.
  • Major beneficiary sectors include textiles, leather, footwear, gems, jewellery, marine products, engineering goods, pharmaceuticals, chemicals, processed food and automobile components.
  • India excludes dairy products, cereals, millets, apples, edible oils and oilseeds from major tariff concessions.
  • This protects small producers, domestic prices and rural livelihoods while balancing liberalisation with policy space for vulnerable sectors.
  • UK commitments cover 12 service sectors and 137 sub-sectors, including IT, finance, engineering, healthcare, education and telecommunications.
  • CETA facilitates temporary movement of business visitors, intra-corporate transferees, contractual service suppliers, independent professionals and investors.
  • The joint statement also recognises the achievements of the Norway–India Partnership Initiative in maternal and child health and its support to India’s National Health Mission.  Oslo hosted the 3rd India–Nordic Summit on May 19, 2026.
  • The summit brought together India with Norway, Denmark, Finland, Iceland and Sweden, and elevated India–Nordic relations to a trusted Green Technology and Innovation Strategic Partnership.
  • On September 11, 2025, India and Norway held their first Maritime Security, Disarmament, and Non- Proliferation Dialogue in Oslo, marking a milestone in their strategic engagement. PM Modi’s Italy Visit
  • Both countries upgraded ties to a Special Strategic Partnership, agreed on annual leaders’ meetings and a Foreign Ministers-led review of the Joint Strategic Action Plan 2025–2029, adopted at the 2024 G20 Summit.

Global Hunger Index 2026 The Global Hunger Index (GHI) 2026 , released in October 2026 under the theme “No Security Without Food Security,” ranked India 100th among 122 countries , with a score of 24.1, placing it in the “serious” hunger category . India’s score improved from 38.1 in 2000 and 37.0 in 2006, but remained worse than the South Asian average of 23.5 and the global average of 18.1.  Key Highlights
  • About GHI: First published in 2006, the Global Hunger Index is an annual, peer-reviewed assessment jointly published by Concern Worldwide, Welthungerhilfe and the Institute for International Law of Peace and Armed Con昀氀ict (IFHV). The 2026 report is its 21st edition.
  • Child wasting remains India’s major nutritional concern, while stunting declined signi昀椀cantly from 50% in 2000 to 29.3% in 2026.  Top Performers: Twenty-four countries, including Armenia, Belarus, Bosnia and Herzegovina, Bulgaria and Chile, recorded GHI scores below 5 and were jointly placed in ranks 1–24.  Lowest-Ranked Countries: Somalia (122nd; 47.0) recorded the highest hunger score, followed by the Democratic Republic of the Congo (121st; 39.5), Chad (120th; 38.0), Madagascar (119th; 36.5) and Haiti (118th; 34.4). Somalia was classi昀椀ed in the “alarming” category.  Global Findings: The global GHI score increased from 17.6 in 2018 to 18.1 in 2026, indicating stalled progress against hunger. South Asia scored 23.5 , remaining in the serious category. Burundi, South Sudan, Sudan and Yemen received provisional alarming designations because of incomplete data.  Major Challenges: Con昀氀ict, climate shocks, economic pressures and disrupted food systems continue to undermine global food security.

India retained its 2025 ranking, & Related Developments

  • India retained its 2025 ranking, while its score improved marginally from 64.4% to 64.5%.
  • Globally, 69.2% of the gender gap has been closed, with full parity estimated to take another 120 years.
  • Launched by the WEF in 2006, the Global Gender Gap Index measures gender disparities rather than absolute development levels.
  • Its scores range from 0 (disparity) to 1 (full parity).
  • The Index assesses Economic Participation and Opportunity, Educational Attainment, Health and Survival, and Political Empowerment.  India’s Performance Dimension Gender Parity Economic Participation and Opportunity 41.2% Educational Attainment 96.6% Health and Survival 95.6% Political Empowerment 24.5% Overall 64.5%
  • Labour: force participation parity stood at 44.1%, while parity among professional and technical workers improved from 26.6% in 2006 to 49.9% in 2026.
  • Representation among legislators, senior officials and managers remained low at 13.1%.
  • India achieved full parity in secondary and tertiary enrolment, while adult literacy parity stood at 82.5%. Health and Survival parity improved to 95.6%.
  • India ranked 67th globally in Political Empowerment with 24.5% parity.
  • Top Five Economies: Iceland (93.0%) ranked first for the 17 th consecutive year, followed by Finland (87.2%), Norway (85.7%), Namibia (84.5%) and the United Kingdom (84.2%).
  • Lowest: Ranked Economies: Chad (145 th), Iran (144th) and Pakistan (143rd).
  • Global Dimension Scores: Educational Attainment (96.9%) and Health and Survival (96.2%) recorded the highest parity, followed by Economic Participation (61.7%) and Political Empowerment (22.1%).
  • Southern Asia ranked 7 th among eight regions, closing 64.5% of its gender gap. FAO Food Price Index The Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) reported that the FAO Food Price Index (FFPI) averaged 131.1 points in July 2026, increasing by 0.7 points (0.6%) from June and 1.0% from July 2025. It reached its highest level since early 2023 but remained 29.1 points (18.2%) below its March 2022 peak. The increase was driven by higher cereal, vegetable oil and sugar prices, partly offset by declines in meat and dairy prices.

The FFPI measures monthly changes & Related Developments

  • The FFPI measures monthly changes in international prices of five globally traded commodity groups— cereals, vegetable oils, meat, dairy products and sugar—weighted by their average export shares during the 2014–2016 base period.
  • It reflects international commodity prices rather than domestic retail food prices.
  • The Cereal Price Index averaged 113.8 points, rising 3.4% monthly and 6.9% annually.
  • Wheat prices increased 5.8% due to Black Sea export disruptions, infrastructure damage and heatwaves affecting production.
  • Maize prices also rose amid hot, dry weather in parts of the United States and higher energy costs.
  • The Vegetable Oil Price Index increased 2.0%, reaching its highest level since June 2022.
  • Higher palm and soybean oil prices reflected strong biodiesel demand, rising crude-oil prices and increased consumption.
  • The Sugar Price Index rose 5.6%, driven by adverse weather in Europe and Asia and expectations of increased sugarcane diversion towards ethanol production in Brazil.
  • In June 2025, in a dramatic escalation of hostilities, Israel launched Operation Rising Lion , a series of coordinated airstrikes and drone attacks targeting Iran’s nuclear and military infrastructure.
  • In response, Iran launched Operation True Promise 3, firing ballistic missiles at Israeli targets, with explosions reported in Jerusalem and Tel Aviv, marking a major regional escalation.
  • In response to growing tensions, the Government of India launched Operation Sindhu.
  • India has a strong track record in such operations, as seen during the 1990–91 Gulf War , the Libya crisis, and Operation Ganga in Ukraine (2022).  Modi Becomes First Leader Honoured by Israel and Palestine
  • PM Narendra Modi became the first leader to receive top honours from both Israel and Palestine.
  • He was awarded the Speaker of the Knesset Medal (Israel’s highest parliamentary honour) on February 25, 2026.

EU Signs Association Agreement with Andorra and San Marino On October 19, 2026, the European Commission, on behalf of the European Union (EU) , signed an Association Agreement with Andorra and San Marino in Brussels, Belgium . It aims to integrate these European microstates into the EU Single Market without granting EU membership.  Key Highlights
  • Nature and Scope: The Mixed Agreement involves the EU, its member states, Andorra and San Marino. It provides for the free movement of goods, services, capital and persons , subject to agreed conditions.
  • Financial Services: Market access will be phased in after regulatory assessments by the European Supervisory Authorities (ESAs) . Cooperation also covers research, education, environment and consumer protection.
  • Negotiation Timeline: Talks began in 2015 under December 2014 Council directives. Negotiations with Monaco were suspended in September 2023, while those with Andorra and San Marino concluded in December 2023.
  • Approval Process: The European Commission submitted proposals on 26 April 2024. The Council of the EU authorised signing and provisional application on 16 July 2026.
  • Legal Status: Under Article 112(3) , provisional application may begin separately with either country after prescribed procedures. Full implementation requires necessary approvals, including European Parliament consent . Provisional application remained uncon昀椀rmed as of 21 October 2026.
  • Monetary Agreements: Andorra ( 2011) and San Marino ( 2012) use the euro through monetary agreements without euro-area membership. They may issue limited national-design euro coins but not banknotes. Updated agreements were also signed.
  • EEA Plus: European Commission Executive Vice- President Maroš Šefčovič informally described the arrangement as “EEA Plus” in December 2023. EEA means European Economic Area; its of昀椀cial legal designation remains Association Agreement. UN Scienti昀椀c Panel Releases First Global AI Assessment 2026 On July 1, 2026, the United Nations-established Independent International Scienti昀椀c Panel on Arti昀椀cial Intelligence (AI) released its 昀椀rst Preliminary Report: Evidence-based Assessment of Opportunities, Risks and Impacts of AI.

The assessment informed the inaugural & Related Developments

  • The assessment informed the inaugural UN Global Dialogue on AI Governance, held in Geneva, Switzerland, on 6–7 July 2026.
  • The report warns that AI capabilities are advancing faster than scientific understanding, independent evaluation and governance safeguards.
  • Created under UN General Assembly Resolution A/RES/79/325 of 26 August 2025, following the Global Digital Compact, the Panel comprises 40 independent experts from all five UN regional groups, appointed for three-year terms.
  • Co-Chairs: Yoshua Bengio (Canada), recipient of the 2018 Turing Award, and Maria Ressa (Philippines), recipient of the 2021 Nobel Peace Prize.
  • The Panel provides independent, non: prescriptive scientific assessments of non-military AI applications; it has no regulatory or enforcement powers.
  • In 2025, US institutions produced 59 notable AI models, China 35 and the rest of the world 13.
  • The private sector accounted for 91% of notable models.

Fiscal & Monetary Affairs Strategic Restructuring of IDBI Bank On October 14, 2026 , the Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs (CCEA) , chaired by the Prime Minister, approved 10955230 Canada Inc., whose parent company is Fairfax Financial Holdings Limited, as the buyer for the strategic sale of a 60.72% stake in IDBI Bank Limited. The approved purchase consideration is approximately ₹52,878.08 crore. This decision marks an important stage in the transfer of the bank’s ownership and management control; completion of the transaction remains subject to compliance with further regulatory conditions.  Background of the Bank
  • IDBI was originally known as the Industrial Development Bank of India . It came into existence as a development 昀椀nance institution on 1 July 1964, and its undertaking was transferred to a banking company with effect from 1 October 2004.
  • IDBI Bank is a scheduled commercial bank regulated by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) . Following LIC’s acquisition of 51% of its paid-up equity capital , the RBI classi昀椀ed it as a private sector bank for regulatory purposes with effect from 21 January 2019. Indicative Shareholding Based on the Approved Main Transaction Shareholder Before the sale Stake to be sold After the main sale Government of India 45.48% 30.48% Approximately 15% LIC 49.24% 30.24% Approximately 19%  Key Features of the Approved Transaction
  • The strategic buyer is 10955230 Canada Inc., whose parent company is Fairfax Financial Holdings Limited.
  • The total approved stake is 60.72%, and the purchase consideration is ₹52,878.08 crore.
  • The Government of India will sell a 30.48% stake, while LIC will sell a 30.24% stake. The sale proceeds will be distributed in the corresponding proportion.
  • The approved bid is ₹81 per share, compared with the benchmark of ₹75.99 per share , calculated as the average of two independent valuations. 57th GST Council Meeting The 57th meeting of the GST Council was held in New Delhi on October 8, 2026 , chaired by Union Minister for Finance and Corporate Affairs Nirmala Sitharaman.

The amendment came into force on October 1, 2026.  Key Details of the Act
  • Section 2 of the Amendment Act substituted the existing Section 13(3) of the principal Act with new subsections 13(3) and 13(3A).
  • The Of昀椀ce of the Registrar General, India , under the Ministry of Home Affairs, appointed 1 October 2026 as the commencement date through Noti昀椀cation S.O. 5081(E), dated 16 September 2026. The noti昀椀cation was issued under Section 1(2) of the Amendment Act.  New Framework for Delayed Registration
  • Information reported after more than two years: Under Section 13(3A) , registration requires an order of a Judicial Magistrate of the First Class.
  • Following the 2023 amendment , registration of all births and deaths reported more than one year after the event required an order of the District Magistrate, Sub-Divisional Magistrate or an authorised Executive Magistrate. The 2026 amendment speci昀椀es different authorities for delays of more than one year but within two years, and more than two years.  Other Provisions

India’s Proposed `10 and `20 Polymer Banknotes The Government of India has approved a proposal by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) for 昀椀eld trials of ₹10 and ₹20 polymer banknotes.  Key Facts
  • The RBI proposed one billion notes of each denomination, totalling 200 crore notes with a face value of approximately ₹3,000 crore. The Government informed Parliament of the approval in 2026, when procurement remained at an initial stage. Regular issuance may be considered only after successful trials.
  • Australia first issued a polymer banknote in 1988. More than 60 countries reportedly use polymer currency, including Canada, the United Kingdom, New Zealand and Singapore.  Currency in Circulation
  • According to the supplied RBI figures, currency in circulation rose 12% year-on-year to ₹41.23 lakh crore (banknotes alone excluding coins) at the end of March 2026 ( ₹40,814 coins in circulation). ₹500 notes represented approximately 86% of banknotes’ total value. Printing expenditure reportedly fell nearly 24% to ₹4,875 crore in 2025–26, mainly because of lower printing requirements. Bankers’ Books Evidence Act, 2026 The Bankers’ Books Evidence Act, 2026 came into force on October 1, 2026. The Act replaces the Bankers’ Books Evidence Act, 1891 with a technology-neutral framework recognising physical, electronic and digi tal banking records as evidence. The de昀椀nition of bankers’ books includes records at off site, virtual, cloud, backup and disaster recovery locations. Banks and bankers also include post of昀椀ce savings bank and money order of昀椀ces.  Legislative Background
  • The first schedule applies to copies of books maintained in written or physical form. The second schedule applies to copies of books maintained electronically, digitally or through other data storage mechanism.

VB – G RAM G Act, 2025 The Viksit Bharat – Guarantee for Rozgar and Ajeevika Mission (Gramin) Act, 2025, or VB-G RAM G Act, is India’s statutory framework for rural wag e employment and village development. It came into force across rural India on July 1, 2026, repealing the Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act, 2005 (MGNREGA).  About the News
  • Rural Development Minister Shivraj Singh Chouhan released over ₹25,000 crore as the first installment, called the Mother Sanction, to support timely wage payments. A total ₹7.5 lakh crore (as on July, 2026) will be invested in 2.86 lakh Panchayats over the next five years. Wage rates have increased by around 10 percent on average.
  • The Act guarantees wage employment, strengthens village economies and promotes sustainable livelihoods. It links employment with productive public assets, transparency, convergence, climate resilience and local economic development, following “Rozgar Bhi, Samman Bhi”, or employment with dignity.  Key Features of the VB-G RAM G Act
  • The Act increases the employment guarantee from 100 to 125 days per financial year for rural households whose adult members volunteer for unskilled manual work.
  • Failure to provide employment within the prescribed timeframe entitles workers to unemployment allowance of at least one-fourth of the notified wage for the first thirty days of the financial year and one-half for the remaining period.
  • Budgetary Commitment: For FY 2026–27, the Central share is ₹95,692.31 crore, described as the largest Budget Estimate allocation for a rural employment programme. Including the estimated State share, total outlay is expected to exceed ₹1.51 lakh crore. Fact Sheet Pilot launch 11 April 2016 FY 2025–26 transaction volume 24,161.69 crore transactions FY 2025–26 transaction value About `314 lakh crore Record monthly volume 2,264 crore transactions in March 2026 Share in India’s digital payments in FY 2025–26 Around 85% Share in global real-time payment volume for 2025 Around 49% April 2026 performance 22.35 billion transactions worth `29.03 lakh crore Income-Tax Act, 2025 The Income-tax Act, 2025 has come into force from 1 April 2026 , replacing the six-decade-old Income- tax Act, 1961. The reform aims to modernise India’s tax system and make it more aligned with current economic realities. It simplifies language, structure and tax compliance while improving the efficiency of ta x administration.  Key Highlights  Operational and Structural Changes
  • The Central Board of Direct Taxes has notified the Income-tax Rules, 2026 to implement the provisions of the new Act.
  • A major procedural reform is the introduction of Form 130, which replaces the traditional Form 16 (TDS certificate issued by employers).
  • The act replaces the concepts of ‘Assessment Year (AY)’ and ‘Previous Year’ with ‘Tax Year’. The Financial Year (FY) continues to remain relevant in the Act.
  • A single unified concept called the “Tax Year” has been introduced for simplification.   • The Ministry of Statistics and Programme Implementation (MoSPI) unveiled its new logo and new mascot on 1 January 2026 to modernise its institutional identity, strengthen public outreach, and reinforce the role of official statistics in nation- building.

  • The upward growth bar conveys progress and explains how credible data supports sustainable economic growth.
  • The colour scheme: saffron, white, green, and deep blue–is used to represent national values such as growth, truth, sustainability, stability, and knowledge, strengthening the credibility of official statistics.

Industrial Sector Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises Development (Amendment) Act, 2026 The Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises Development (Amendment) Act, 2026 modernises the Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises Development Act, 2006. It seeks to address delayed payments, improve MSME liquidity, simplify digital registration, strengthen dispute-resolution institutions and rationalise compliance-related penalties.  Legislative Background and Present Status
  • It amends speci昀椀c provisions of the MSMED Act, 2006 rather than replacing the entire principal legislation.  Key Provisions of the MSMED (Amendment) Bill, 2026
  • Discounted invoices increased from ₹40,000 crore in 2021–22 to ₹3.47 lakh crore in 2025–26.

MeiTY announced the noti昀椀cation of MPMS on August 21, 2026 after the Cabinet approval. The scheme has a budgetary outlay of ₹62,500 crore and seeks to strengthen India’s mobile phone manufacturing ecosystem.  About the Scheme
  • MPMS aims to expand mobile phone production, deepen domestic value addition, strengthen supply- chain resilience and improve global competitiveness.
  • The scheme will operate for 昀椀ve years, from FY 2026–27 to FY 2030–31.
  • It succeeds the Production Linked Incentive Scheme for Large-Scale Electronics Manufacturing (PLI-LSEM), which ended on 31 March 2026.
  • Manufacturers will receive incentives ranging from 2.25% to 5% on eligible sales of mobile phones manufactured in India.
  • An additional incentive of up to 1.5% will be provided for domestic sourcing of key components and sub-assemblies.
  • India’s electronics production increased from ₹1.90 lakh crore in 2014–15 to an estimated ₹13.11 lakh crore in 2025–26, representing nearly seven-fold growth.
  • Electronics exports increased from ₹38,263 crore to ₹4.24 lakh crore during the same period, registering an eleven-fold rise.
  • Electronic goods became India’s third-largest export category, reaching USD 47.96 billion in FY 2025–26.

Global real GDP grew by & Related Developments

  • Global real GDP grew by 3.5% in 2025 . Growth is projected at 3.1% in 2026 and 3.4% in 2027 . Compared with April 2026, the forecast for 2026 remains unchanged, while the forecast for 2027 has been raised by 0.2 percentage points.

Global Multidimensional Poverty Index 2026 The Global Multidimensional Poverty Index, 2026, released on October 12, 2026, is jointly published by the UN Development Programme (UNDP) and the Oxford Poverty and Human Development Initiative (OPHI).  Key Highlights of the Report
  • The 2026 Global Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) report provides a statistical update of the Global Multidimensional Poverty Index, covering 108 countries.
  • The report highlights that, of the 6.3 billion people living in these countries, 1.2 billion people - or 18.9 per cent - are living in acute multidimensional poverty.
  • The number of people living in multidimensional poverty has risen for the 昀椀rst time since the index was launched in 2010.
  • Sub: Saharan Africa has both the highest poverty incidence (51.4%) and the largest poor population (616 million). South Asia follows with approximately 393 million poor people, with poverty incidence of 19.9%.
  • Highest poverty: Chad, with an MPI of 0.517 and a poverty incidence of 84.2%.
  • The 昀椀ve countries with the highest MPI values , arranged from highest to lowest, are: Country MPI value Poverty incidence Chad 0.517 84.2% Niger 0.470 79.9% Central African Republic 0.461 80.4% Burundi 0.409 75.1% Madagascar 0.386 68.4%  India Speci昀椀c Highlights
  • The report estimates that 237.849 million people (approximately 23.78 crore) as multidimensi- onally poor based on the 2024 population , the largest absolute number among the countries covered.
  • FCNR(B) deposits accounted for the largest share, attracting $65.40 billion.
  • The ISP is India’s first high: frequency macroeconomic indicator measuring short-term changes in the volume of formal services output relative to a base year. It fills a statistical gap because the Index of Industrial Production does not capture changes in services activity.  First Trial Release

NITI Aayog Report on Regulatory Reforms in Professional Services On August 10, 2026 , NITI Aayog Vice-Chairman Ashok Kumar Lahiri launched India’s Services Sector: Insights on Regulatory Regime in Professional Services . It is the third report under the Services Thematic Series; the related PIB release was published on August 14, 2026. It identi昀椀es reforms that could improve regulatory clarity, professional mobility, recognition of quali昀椀cations and international competitiveness.  Four-Pronged Strategy 1. Continuous Professional Development for Professionals: Structured CPD and lifelong learning can maintain professional competence as technology and industry practices change. 2. Adoption of Best Practices in Professional Services: Regulators should publish clear eligibility rules, contact details, processing timelines, decisions and review mechanisms and should hold regular consultations. 3. Elevate Professional Services within the Services Value Chain: India should move from routine, cost-based delivery towards research, engineering design, legal analytics, advanced 昀椀nancial modelling and strategic consulting. 4. Advance Emerging Trends in Professional Services: Professionals should build capabilities in arti昀椀cial intelligence, automation, ESG reporting, carbon accounting, climate-risk assessment, sustainable 昀椀nance and supply-chain advisory. The report cautions that adopting best practices d oes not mean mechanically copying foreign laws. SUMAN Roadmap 2030 Union Health Minister Jagat Prakash Nadda launched the roadmap on June 29, 2026, at the 16 th Conference of the Central Council of Health and Family Welfare.
  • The roadmap is an evidence: driven framework of the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, led by its Maternal Health Division under RMNCHA+N, integrating reproductive, maternal, newborn, child and adolescent health and nutrition.
  • It aims to achieve the SDG target of an MMR below 70 per 100,000 live births by 2030, reduce neonatal and infant mortality, ensure full service coverage across all States and Union Territories, and achieve zero preventable maternal and newborn deaths.
  • The SUMAN package promotes early registration, complete antenatal care, quality assessment and adequate institutional stay after delivery. Interventions target 130 districts across 13 States.
  • SUMAN Panchayats promote antenatal care, institutional delivery, immunisation and zero maternal and infant deaths. “Mothers’ Picnic” promotes awareness.
  • Maharashtra accounted for the highest share (39%) of total FDI equity inflows in FY 2024–25, followed by Karnataka (13%) and Delhi (12%).

  • Transfers to States will increase to `26.21 lakh crore, including `15.26 lakh crore as tax devolution and `10.94 lakh crore as grants and loans.
  • Capital expenditure loans to States amount to `1.85 lakh crore.  Overall Assessment
  • Total receipts, excluding borrowings, are estimated at `36.51 lakh crore , reflecting a 7.2% increase over 2025–26 (RE) , mainly driven by higher net tax revenue.
  • Total expenditure is estimated at `53.47 lakh crore, an increase of 7.7% over 2025–26 (RE).
  • Central Sector Schemes account for `17.72 lakh crore, while Centrally Sponsored Schemes (CSS) account for `9.90 lakh crore , with CSS showing a higher growth rate of 17.1%.  Committed Expenditure and Transfers
  • Pension expenditure in 2026: 27 is estimated at `2.96 lakh crore, rising modestly by 3%.
  • Interest payments are projected at `14.04 lakh crore, consuming over one-fourth of total expenditure.
  • Special loans to States for capital expenditure amount to `1.85 lakh crore , supporting state-level capex.
  • Finance Commission grants for 2026: 27 are estimated at `1.29 lakh crore, about 15% lower than 2025–26 (RE), as per 16th Finance Commission recommendations.  Expenditure by Major Ministries
  • The Ministry of Defence has the highest allocation at `7.85 lakh crore , constituting 15% of total expenditure.
  • The Ministry of Defence allocation increases by `52,166 crore (7%) in 2026–27 over 2025–26 (RE), reflecting a sustained focus on national security.
  • Capital outlay for defence services rises sharply to `2,19,306 crore , marking a 17.6% increase , reinforcing indigenisation and modernisation of the armed forces.  Subsidy Expenditure
  • Total subsidy expenditure in 2026: 27 is estimated at `4,54,773 crore , representing a 3.1% decline from 2025–26 (RE).

  • VB-G RAM G receives the highest allocation of `95,692 crore in 2026–27, following the replacement of MGNREGA by the VB-G RAM G Act in December 2025.

  • The Survey argues that India’s scale population of 145 crore, and democracy make it unrealistic to copy external development templates in a geopolitically uncertain world.
  • The First Advance Estimates project real GDP growth at 7.4% and GVA growth at 7.3% for FY26, sustaining India’s position as the fastest-growing major economy for the fourth consecutive year.
  • Private Final Consumption Expenditure rises by 7.0% in FY26 and reaches 61.5% of GDP , the highest share since 2012 (also seen in FY23), supported by low inflation , stable employment, and rising real purchasing power.

The Survey states that credible & Related Developments

  • The Survey states that credible fiscal consolidation has reinforced macro-fiscal confidence and is linked with three sovereign credit rating upgrades in 2025 by Morningstar DBRS, S&P Global Ratings, and R&I.
  • The Centre’s revenue receipts strengthen to 9.2% of GDP in FY25 (PA) , up from an average of about 8.5% of GDP during FY16–FY20.
  • Non: corporate tax collections have risen structurally, from about 2.4% of GDP in the pre-pandemic period to around 3.3% in the post- pandemic period.
  • The direct tax base expands, with income tax returns filed increasing from 6.9 crore (FY22) to 9.2 crore (FY25), reflecting higher compliance and technology-enabled administration.
  • Gross GST collections during April: December 2025 stand at `17.4 lakh crore, recording 6.7% YoY growth, while e-way bill volumes grow 21% YoY in the same period, indicating robust transactions.
  • The Centre’s effective capital expenditure increases to about 4.0% of GDP in FY25, up from a pre-pandemic average of 2.7% and post-pandemic level of about 3.9%.
  • Through Special Assistance to States for Capital Expenditure (SASCI) , states are incentivised to maintain capital spending at around 2.4% of GDP in FY25.
  • The combined State fiscal deficit edges up to 3.2% of GDP in FY25 , from about 2.8% in the post- pandemic period, reflecting emerging pressures.
  • Under PMJDY, 55.02 crore bank accounts are opened as of March 2025, including 36.63 crore in rural and semi-urban areas.
  • Financial participation broadened as unique investors crossed 12 crore in September 2025 , with nearly 25% being women.
  • During FY26 (till December 2025) , 235 lakh demat accounts are added, taking total demat accounts beyond 21.6 crore.
  • The mutual fund investor base expanded to 5.9 crore unique investors (December 2025) , with significant participation from non-Tier I and Tier II locations.
  • India remained the world’s largest recipient of remittances, with inflows of USD 135.4 billion in FY25, helping offset the merchandise trade deficit.
  • India rank 4th globally in Greenfield investment announcements (2024) and emerges as the largest destination for Greenfield digital investments (2020–24).  In昀氀ation: Tamed and Anchored
  • Agricultural marketing efficiency improved through AMI under ISAM and the Agriculture Infrastructure Fund , while e-NAM enhanced price discovery by onboarding 1.79 crore farmers , 2.72 crore traders , and 4,698 FPOs across 1,522 mandis by December 31, 2025.
  • Farmers’ incomes were supported through assured MSP, PM-KISAN, and PM-Kisan Maandhan Yojana, with `4.09 lakh crore disbursed under PM-KISAN and 24.92 lakh farmers enrolled in PMKMY.  Services Sector: From Stability to New Frontiers
  • India became the world’s seventh: largest services exporter, with its share in global services trade rising from 2% in 2005 to 4.3% in 2024.
  • PLI Schemes across 14 sectors attracted over `2.0 lakh crore investment, generated `18.7 lakh crore of incremental production, and created over 12.6 lakh jobs.
  • The India Semiconductor Mission advanced domestic capabilities with 10 approved projects involving `1.60 lakh crore investment across 6 States.  Investment and Infrastructure
  • Central government capital expenditure increased 4.2 times, from `2.63 lakh crore in FY18 to `11.21 lakh crore in FY26 (BE) , with effective capex at `15.48 lakh crore.

  • India operates one of the world’s largest school systems, serving 24.69 crore students across 14.71 lakh schools.
  • Total employment reached 56.2 crore in Q2 FY26, with net job creation continuing.

Science & Technology Affairs

Space exploration missions (JAXA MMX, Artemis II, ISRO LVM3), indigenous defence systems, health research, and AI.

JAXA Martian Moons eXploration (MMX) & Related Developments

  • JAXA Martian Moons eXploration (MMX) mission The Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency (JAXA) launched the Martian Moons eXploration (MMX) mission on 20 October 2026 aboard H3 Flight No. 10 from Tanegashima Space Center, Japan . It aims to achieve the world’s 昀椀rst sample return from the Martian system. Its planned duration is approximately 昀椀ve years.  Key Highlights
  • Mission objectives: Study Phobos and Deimos, Mars’s two moons, and return at least 10 grams of material from Phobos.
  • Timeline: Arrival at Mars is expected in 2027, and sample delivery to Earth in 2031.
  • Scientific significance: Determine whether the moons are captured asteroids or debris from a giant impact on Mars , improving understanding of planetary formation and the delivery of water and organic compounds.  Technical Features
  • Spacecraft: Three modules—propulsion, exploration and return—use solar panels for electricity and chemical propulsion for manoeuvres.
  • Orbit: A quasi-satellite orbit around Mars near Phobos supports surface mapping and landing-site selection.
  • Instruments: The spacecraft carries 13 mission instruments. TENGOO provides approximately 40 cm imaging resolution ; OROCHI captures images at multiple wavelengths; LIDAR measures surface distance using laser pulses.
  • Spectrometers: NASA ’s MEGANE (Mars-moon Exploration with Gamma rays and Neutrons) measures gamma rays and neutrons to determine elemental composition. MIRS (MMX InfraRed Spectrometer) operates at 0.9–3.6 micrometres , investigating minerals, water-related substances and organic matter.
  • Communication and return: NASA ’s Deep Space Network supports communication and navigation. A Sample Return Capsule will bring samples through Earth’s atmosphere; NASA will receive about 15% for research and curation. First GAGAN-Based Satellite In late June 2026, the Directorate General of Civil Aviation (DGCA) supervised India’s 昀椀rst Satellite-Based Landing System (SLS) approach by a commercial jet using the indigenous GAGAN system. An IndiGo Airbus A321 conducted the demonstration using satellite-derived guidance instead of relying on a conventional Instrument Landing System (ILS).  Key Highlights
  • The spacecraft was launched on September 24, 2025, at 7:30 a.m. EDT from Launch Complex 39A at NASA ’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.
  • Surface Erosion and Compaction: The engine 昀椀ring blew away the top 3 cm of regolith, exposing and slightly compacting the material underneath. India’s First Orbital Data Centre Satellite Path昀椀nder Recently on 4 May 2026 , Pixxel announced a strategic partnership with Sarvam to develop and build Path昀椀nder , described as India’s 昀椀rst orbital data centre satellite . Pixxel will design, build, launch and operate the satellite, while Sarvam will provide th e AI backbone for running full-stack language models, training and inference directly in orbit. The mission is important because it brings together remote sensing, high-performance computing and India-built AI on a single satellite platform.  Orbital Data Centre

Path昀椀nder is a single & Related Developments

  • Path昀椀nder is a single: satellite demonstrator . Its purpose is to test whether powerful computing hardware, similar to that used in ground data centres, can work reliably in the space environment.  Role of Pixxel and Sarvam
  • Pixxel’s role:  Pixxel will design, build, launch and operate Path昀椀nder. It will also provide the hyperspectral imaging payload.  The satellite will be developed at Gigapixxel, Pixxel’s upcoming facility designed to scale satellite production to up to 100 units.
  • Sarvam’s role:  Sarvam will provide the AI backbone. Its full- stack sovereign AI platform, including models and inference infrastructure developed and governed in India, will run directly on the satellite’s GPU layer. Artemis II Mission Artemis II was not a Moon-landing mission; it was a crewed lunar flyby mission meant to prove that NASA ’s Space Launch System, Orion spacecraft, ground systems, recovery teams and mission procedures can safely carry humans beyond low Earth orbit and bring them back. NASA describes it as the first crewed Artemis flight and a key step toward long-term human return to the Moon and future crewed missions to Mars.
  • Artemis II lifted off from the Kennedy Space Center on April 1, 2026, sending the first humans to the Moon since 1972. (Note: The Apollo 17 mission in 1972 sent humans to land on the Moon, whereas Artemis II sent humans toward the Moon.)
  • During the mission, the crew completed a lunar flyby, returning humans to the Moon’s vicinity for the first time in 53 years after the final Apollo Mission to the Moon.  Engineering Focus of the Mission
  • The Artemis II Orion heat shield is the critical thermal protection system on the base of Orion Spacecraft. Orion heat shield protect the Capsule and Astronauts from searing temperatures and Extreme friction during high speed atmospheric re-entry.
  • During the flight, the astronauts tested Orion’s life- support, communications, control, navigation and manual operation systems in a deep-space environment.
  • The mission also tested how well astronauts could live and work inside Orion during a lunar-return flight.
  • The conference was inaugurated on 8 April 2026 by Shri A. S. Kiran Kumar, former Chairman of ISRO and former Secretary, Department of Space. India’s First Hydrogen-Powered Train Indian Railways is entering a new era of clean and sustainable transportation. Hydrogen fuel cell technology has emerged globally as a promising alternative to fossil fuel-based traction systems. India’s 昀椀rst indigenous hydrogen-powered train marks an important step in adopting this next-generation technology. It is a locomotive that generates its own electricity onboa rd using hydrogen. With the inauguration on the Jind- Sonipat section ( Haryana) of the Northern Railway on 17 July 2026 by PM Narendra Modi ,the hydrogen fuel cell-powered train is begin its operations.

It will demonstrate the feasibility & Related Developments

  • It will demonstrate the feasibility of clean rail transportation in India.  Inside India’s First Hydrogen Train
  • The hydrogen train project has been developed in accordance with design approval and technical speci昀椀cations prepared by the Research, Designs & Standards Organisation (RDSO) . Designed and developed entirely in India, the project demonstrates the vision of Atmanirbhar Bharat.  Key Features of the Hydrogen Powered Train on the Jind- Sonipat Section-
  • The Train has two 1200 kW power cars, totalling 2400 kW hydrogen fuel cell propulsion system.
  • Approved to operate at a maximum speed of 75 kmph with a design speed of 110 kmph.
  • Capacity of Around 2,600 Passengers.
  • Developed from the design stage through prototype manufacturing.
  • It will connect Jind Junction, Gohana Junction and Sonipat while serving intermediate stations.  Technology Behind the Train
  • Hydrogen fuel cell technology produces electricity through a chemical reaction using hydrogen.
  • The country’s largest railway hydrogen storage and refuelling facility has been established at Jind, Haryana.
  • Each DPC houses fuel cells, lithium iron phosphate (LFP) batteries and hydrogen storage cylinders. PFBR at Kalpakkam, Tamil Nadu India has marked a major milestone in its nuclear energy programme. The indigenously designed and built Prototype Fast Breeder Reactor (PFBR) at Kalpakkam in Tamil Nadu successfully attained its first criticality on 6th April 2026, marking the initiation of a sustained nuclear chain reaction. This PFBR is a 500 MWe (megawatt electrical) reactor built by Bharatiya Nabhikiya Vidyut Nigam Limited (BHAVINI) at the Kalpakkam Nuclear Complex.
  • With this achievement, India has officially entered the second stage of its three-stage nuclear power programme, a vision first conceived by Dr. Homi Jehangir Bhabha, the architect of India’s nuclear programme.
  • The milestone carries substantial global significance. Once fully operational, India will become only the second country in the world after Russia to operate a commercial fast breeder reactor.  India’s First Dedicated Quantum & AI University Campus

JAMA Blood Group in Humans The International Society of Blood Transfusion (ISBT) has recognised JAMA as the 49 th human blood group system, numbered ISBT 049. The discovery followed a joint investigation by researchers in the United Kingdom and Israel into unusual blood samples from a woman and her brother. ISBT rati昀椀ed the system at its Kuala Lumpur Congress in 2026 and announced it on September 14, 2026. The 昀椀nding adds to the understanding of rare blood characteristics and the ir possible importance in transfusion medicine.  Key Highlights
  • Blood plays a vital role in the human body, carrying oxygen and essential nutrients to cells. For years, A, B, AB and O have been the blood groups most familiar to people.
  • Now, scientists have identi昀椀ed a new blood group system called JAMA, taking the total number of recognised blood group systems to 49.
  • The International Society of Blood Transfusion (ISBT) has of昀椀cially recognised JAMA as the 49th blood group system.
  • The discovery came from a joint investigation by researchers from the UK and Israel. It began when scientists examined the blood of an Israeli woman and her brother.
  • The woman was found to have an antibody that reacted with the red blood cells of most people.
  • However, the same reaction did not occur with her own red blood cells or those of her brother.
  • This unusual result caught researchers’ attention because the antibody did not correspond to any previously identi昀椀ed blood group antigen.
  • The Global Strategic Plan to End Human Deaths from Dog-Mediated Rabies by 2030. Takeda’s TAK-003 (Qdenga) Recently CDSCO’s subject expert committee recommended Takeda’s TAK-003 (Qdenga), its first dengue vaccine, cleared by the Subject Expert Committee (SEC) under the Drugs Controller General of India (DCGI) for individuals aged 4 to 60 years marking a historic shift from reactive vector control to preventive public health strategy.

India’s dengue control strategy is & Related Developments

  • India’s dengue control strategy is entering a vaccine- assisted phase. The official NCVBDC table now records 2,89,235 cases and 485 deaths in 2023, 2,33,519 cases and 297 deaths in 2024, 1,21,824 cases and 131 deaths in 2025, and 6,927 cases with 10 deaths provisionally till 28 February 2026.  TAK-003(Qdenga)
  • TAK: 003(Qdenga), is a live-attenuated vaccine containing weakened versions of dengue virus serotypes 1, 2, 3 and 4 developed by Takeda Pharma (A Japanese pharmaceutical company).
  • TAK: 003 uses the DENV2 strain as the genomic backbone.
  • The vaccine schedule is a 2: dose series three months apart, given to specific age groups and in specific circumstances according to WHO recommenda - tions. SAHI & BODH Health AI Initiatives India has taken an important step in digital health governance with the launch of two new initiatives on 17 February 2026 at the India AI Impact Summit 2026 — SAHI (Strategy for Arti昀椀cial Intelligence in Healthcare for India) and BODH (Benchmarking Open Data Platform for Health AI). These initiatives are meant to make the use of arti昀椀cial intelligence in healthcare safe, ethical, evidence-based, transparent and inclusive.  SAHI: a national framework for responsible Health AI
  • SAHI is a national guidance framework for the adoption of AI across India’s healthcare system. It gives strategic direction on governance, data stewardship, validation, deployment and monitoring of AI tools.
  • An important feature of SAHI is that it is designed to create coordination between healthcare institutions, technology developers, researchers and policymakers.  BODH: benchmarking and validation platform for Health AI
  • BODH has been developed by the Indian Institute of Technology Kanpur in collaboration with the National Health Authority.
  • It is a privacy: preserving benchmarking platform for Health AI. Its main role is to allow rigorous evaluation of AI models on diverse, anonymized real- world health data without sharing the underlying datasets.  AI-Preneurs of India

Chapter 6

Environmental Affairs

UNEP Emissions Gap Report 2026, Ramsar wetlands tally (103), COP30 climate negotiations, and biodiversity initiatives.

UNEP’s Emissions Gap Report 2026 & Related Developments

  • UNEP’s Emissions Gap Report 2026 UNEP’s Emissions Gap Report 2026: 17th edition of the Emissions Gap Report 2026 (titled The Point of Return) was released by the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) on October 21, 2026.
  • Global emissions: Greenhouse gas emissions reached 58.4 gigatonnes of CO 2 equivalent (GtCO2e) in 2025, against a revised 58.5 GtCO2e in 2024; uncertainty prevents confirming a decline or peak.
  • G20: Excluding the Afrizcan Union, members contributed 76% of global emissions; their emissions excluding land use, land-use change and forestry rose 0.7% in 2025.
  • National commitments: By 1 September 2026 , 124 Parties, representing 76% of global emissions, had submitted updated nationally determined contributions (NDCs).
  • Warming threshold: Exceeding 1.5°C above preindustrial levels is unavoidable; stronger mitigation can reduce its magnitude and duration.
  • Lowest overshoot pathway: Peak warming can be limited to 1.7–1.8°C with 66% probability , with around 50% probability of returning to 1.5°C by 2100.
  • Required reductions: Against 2025 levels , emissions must fall 18% by 2030, 45% by 2035, and 69% by 2040.
  • Commitment shortfall: Full NDC implementation delivers only 5–8% reductions by 2030 and 11–15% by 2035.
  • Third: largest emitter: India’s greenhouse gas emissions were 4.5 billion tonnes of CO 2 equivalent in 2025, placing it third after China and the United States. This estimate excludes land use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF).
  • Low per capita emissions: India emitted 3.1 tonnes of CO 2 equivalent per person in 2025 , compared with the global average of 6.4 tonnes— the lowest among the six largest emitters assessed. These figures also exclude LULUCF.

Total Ramsar sites in India & Related Developments

  • Total Ramsar sites in India at Present: 103
  • State having the most number of Ramsar sites: Tamil Nadu (20)
  • Largest wetland under the Ramsar convention (in India): Sundarbans, West Bengal.
  • Smallest wetland under the Ramsar convention (in India): Vembannur Wetland Complex Tamil Nadu
  • First wetland under the Ramsar convention (in India): Chilika Lake and Keoladeo National Park (Rajasthan) (1981) Major Ramsar Sites in India 1. Sukhna Wetland (March 16, 2026)  Key Highlights Background
  • The Montreal Protocol (1987) was adopted to phase out CFCs and other ozone-depleting substances.
  • Countries have since eliminated nearly 99% of controlled ozone-depleting substances, helping the ozone layer recover.
  • HFCs became widely used in refrigeration and air- conditioning because they do not damage the ozone layer.
  • The Kigali Amendment entered into force in January 2019 . It establishes binding schedules to reduce HFC production and consumption, while developing countries receive longer compliance periods.
  • India ratified the Kigali Amendment in 2021.  2028: Freeze HFC production and consumption at the agreed baseline.  2032: Reduce HFCs by 10% below the baseline.  2047: Achieve an 85% reduction.
  • Equipment purchased before the first reductions may remain in use well into the 2030s. Cabinet approves Green Energy Corridor Phase-III scheme On 30 September 2026, the Union Cabinet approved Green Energy Corridor Phase III to strengthen renewable electricity transmission and add battery storage.  Key Highlights  Green Energy Corridor

It aims to support evacuation & Related Developments

  • It aims to support evacuation of up to 135 gigawatts (GW) of renewable energy and deployment of 50 gigawatt-hours (GWh) of Battery Energy Storage Systems (BESS) by FY 2032–33.  Key Highlights  103rd Ramsar Site: Sauj Lake
  • Sauj Lake, located in Mainpuri district of Uttar Pradesh, is a shallow lake surrounded by farmland in the floodplain of the Ganga River.
  • It forms part of a larger network of wetlands, contributing to ecological connectivity in the wider landscape, and is close to Saman Bird Sanctuary.  102nd Ramsar Site: Sukhna Wetland
  • Located at the edge of the city of Chandigarh and at the base of the Outer Himalayas , Sukhna Wetland is a peri-urban reservoir that plays an important role in regulating sediment and water flows from the surrounding catchment.  101st Ramsar Site: Glaw Lake
  • Glaw Lake in Arunachal Pradesh was announced as India’s 101st Ramsar Site on 3 August 2026.
  • It became the first wetland from Arunachal Pradesh to be included in the List of Wetlands of International Importance.
  • Glaw Lake, locally known as Glaw Tuwi, is a natural freshwater wetland in Lohit district.
  • As of August 2026, India ranked third globally by number of Ramsar Sites, behind the United Kingdom with 176 sites and Mexico with 144 sites.  100th Ramsar Site: Jai Prakash Narayan Bird Sanctuary (Surha Tal)
  • On 5 June 2026, the Jai Prakash Narayan Bird Sanctuary, also known as Surha Tal, located in Ballia district of Uttar Pradesh, was recognised as India’s 100th Ramsar Site. India’s Net Zero Portal and the NAPCC Dashboard Union Minister of State for Environment, Forest and Climate Change, Shri Kirti Vardhan Singh, on 16 September, 2026 launched India’s Net Zero Portal and the National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC) Dashboard on the occasion of World Ozone Day 2026 in Mumbai. The two digital initiatives mark an important step towards strengthening transparency, coordination and evidence-based climate action.  Key Highlights
  • Net Zero emissions refer to a state where anthropogenic greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by an entity (organization / industry / government) over a period of one year are compensated by GHG removals through anthropogenic interventions over the same period of time.  The Net Zero Portal
  • The Net Zero Portal is a centralised digital platform for voluntary registration and disclosure of Net Zero commitments by Indian entities across sectors.
  • Participating entities can disclose their greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, declare Net Zero targets and provide annual progress updates.  The NAPCC Dashboard
  • The NAPCC Dashboard provides a consolidated digital view of implementation and progress across the National Missions under the NAPCC.
  • It brings together mission: related information and identified indicators on a common platform, facilitating systematic monitoring by concerned Ministries and Departments.  Indian climate goals in 2015
  • India’s 2015 Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC) contained eight goals. Three were quantitative targets, shown in the comparison below.
  • The remaining five concerned sustainable lifestyles, cleaner development, adaptation, finance and technology .  NDC updates in 2022 and 2026
  • The 2022 Updated First NDC strengthened India’s targets for 2030 and explicitly supported Lifestyle for Environment (LiFE). India submitted its NDC for 2031–2035 on 24 April 2026, raising all three quantitative targets.   • The Great Indian Bustard is a flagship grassland bird and an indicator of healthy grassland ecosystems. Pradhan Mantri Surya Sarovar Yojana The Union Cabinet, chaired by Prime Minister Narendra Modi, approved the Pradhan Mantri Surya Sarovar Yojana (PM-SSY) to accelerate 昀氀oating solar development. It combines Floating Solar Photovoltaic (FSPV) projects with co-located Energy Storage Systems (ESS) to support land-ef昀椀cient generation, grid 昀氀exibility and peak-demand management.  Background and Need
  • PM-SSY covers all States and Union Territories and seeks to add 5,000 MW of floating-solar capacity. Eligible projects will receive Central Financial Assistance of ₹1 crore per MW after successful commissioning. Feasibility studies, including bathymetry, hydrography and environmental studies, may receive up to ₹50 lakh per project.
  • Installed solar capacity increased more than 58 times, from 2.82 GW in March 2014 to 164.59 GW on 31 July 2026, making solar the largest individual component of renewable capacity. This included 122.57 GW ground-mounted, 30.74 GW grid- connected rooftop, 4.77 GW in hybrid projects and 6.51 GW off-grid solar.  Record Growth in FY 2025 to 2026
  • The PLI Scheme for High: Efficiency Solar PV Modules began in 2021 with an initial outlay of ₹4,500 crore, followed by a ₹19,500 crore second tranche. By March 2026, reported cumulative investment was approximately ₹64,873 crore and direct employment 14,794. By 10 June 2026, domestic module and cell manufacturing capacities reached about 192 GW and 30 GW respectively. Module capacity enlisted under the Approved List of Models and Manufacturers (ALMM) crossed 200 GW in August 2026; this category differs from total manufacturing capacity.  Current Solar and Non Fossil Capacity Snapshot
  • On 31 July 2026, renewable capacity including large hydro was 291.73 GW . Total non-fossil capacity was 300.50 GW , comprising solar 164.59 GW , wind 58.14 GW , large and small hydro 57.24 GW , bio- power 11.75 GW and nuclear 8.78 GW . The nuclear figure excludes 100 MW under prolonged outage, temporarily removed from installed-capacity calculations from 31 May 2025. Non-fossil sources exceeded 54% of approximately 552 GW total capacity. A rounded 27 August update placed non- fossil capacity at 302–303 GW and solar at around 165 GW . The detailed 31 July break-up remains the source-wise statistical reference available up to 30 August 2026. India launched its 昀椀rst-ever Guide to Grasslands India launched its first-ever Guide to Grasslands and Other Open Natural Ecosystems (ONEs) of India on August 17, 2026 at an official side event of the 17th Conference of the Parties (COP17) to the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification (UNCCD) in Ulaanbaatar, Mongolia.  Key Highlights
  • The Guide was launched under the aegis and guidance of the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change.

Grasslands, scrublands and ravines have & Related Developments

  • Grasslands, scrublands and ravines have often been assessed mainly on the basis of their lack of dense tree cover and consequently described as “wastelands”.
  • However, these ecosystems provide habitats to specialised and endemic species and sustain pastoral and livestock-dependent communities.
  • Major Open Natural Ecosystems covered in the Guide  Himalayan and Terai grasslands  Thar and Banni landscapes  Deccan savannas  Rocky plateaus  Ravines and scrublands  Coastal ecosystems  Desert landscapes UNCCD COP17
  • Mongolia hosted UNCCD COP17 in Ulaanbaatar from 17–28 August 2026 under the theme “Restoring Land, Restoring Hope.” International Year of Rangelands and Pastoralists 2026
  • The UN General Assembly proclaimed 2026 as the International Year of Rangelands and Pastoralists (IYRP) through Resolution 76/253, adopted on 15 March 2022.
  • The proposal was spearheaded by Mongolia, while the Food and Agriculture Organization is the lead agency for its implementation. India’s First Dedicated Urban Night Safari The Supreme Court on 15 July 2026 permitted Uttar Pradesh to proceed with the proposed Kukrail Night Safari and Zoological Park Project in Lucknow.However, this is only conditional prior permission from the Court. Prakriti Café is a workplace food outlet that brings the principles of Ayush Ahaar into everyday meal choices.  Key Highlights
  • E5, E10 and E20 contain about 5%, 10% and 20% ethanol by volume. E20 combines anhydrous ethanol with 80% petrol. Since November 2023, the Ethanol Supply Year (ESY) runs from 1 November to 31 October. India first achieved 20% blending in 2025; the November 2025 – August 2026 average was 20%. Public-sector outlets normally supply E20; premium grades XP100, poWer100 and Speed100 remain ethanol-free. From 1 April 2026, notified blended petrol requires a minimum Research Octane Number of 95.  Evolution of the Programme
  • Pilots began in 2001, and the EBP Programme launched in 2003 in nine States and four Union Territories. The 2018 National Policy on Biofuels targeted E20 by 2030; the 2022 amendment advanced it to ESY 2025–26. E10 was achieved in June 2022, and phased E20 rollout began on 6 February 2023.  Scale and Measurable Outcomes
  • Capacity grew from 421 crore litres in 2014 to about 2,000 crore litres in 2026. Procurement was about 38 crore litres in ESY 2013–14 and was projected to exceed 1,200 crore litres in ESY 2025–26. The 10 July 2026 update reported cumulative gains since ESY 2014–15: foreign-exchange savings exceeding ₹1.97 lakh crore, nearly 316 lakh tonnes of crude substitution, around 952 lakh tonnes of CO 2 reduction and farmer payments exceeding ₹1.66 lakh crore.

The initiative aims to restore & Related Developments

  • The initiative aims to restore fish habitats and strengthen the resilience of coastal communities.
  • The project is jointly funded by the Central and State governments in a 60:40 ratio. Sustainable Development Report 2026 and SDG Progress in India The Sustainable Development Report, 2026, released by the Sustainable Development Solutions Network (SDSN) on June 23, 2026, places India 94 th among 169 ranked countries with a score of 68.3 out of 100. On the SDSN scale, a score of 100 would indicate achievement of all the SDGs. India has climbed 18 places, from 112th in 2015 to 94 th in 2026 , making it one of the largest movers since the SDGs were adopted. East and South Asia have recorded the strongest regional progress since 2015. Among major economies, India improved by 18 places, while China improved by 14 places.  Key Highlights
  • India’s higher score has not yet placed the country on course to meet the 2030 Agenda. According to the 2026 assessment.
  • A calculation from the official 2026 data set finds trends for 75 available indicators for India :  Of these 25 are on track or maintaining achievement (33.3%).  Eight are moderately improving (10.7%), 33 are stagnating (44.0%) and Nine are decreasing (12.0%)  Thus moderate improvement and stagnation together account for 54.7%.  These are indicator shares, not shares of all 169 targets.  Zero Hunger and India’s Double Burden of Malnutrition
  • SDG 2: Zero Hunger remains one of India’s most serious development challenges. India’s overall performance under this goal is largely stagnating.
  • Of the 9 indicators assessed under SDG 2, 4 remain in the “significant challenges” or “major challenges” categories.
  • The only SDG 2 indicator presently on track is cereal yield, which reached 3.6 tonnes per hectare of harvested land in 2023 . Progress on nutrition- related indicators has been much slower.  Child stunting
  • Stunting, which refers to low height for age and usually reflects long-term undernutrition, declined from 37.9 per cent in 2015 to 35.5 per cent in 2020 in the series used by the SDG report.  Child wasting

E85 Fuel Rollout in India India’s launch of E85 fuel marks a shift from ordinary ethanol blending in petrol to a wider 昀氀ex-fuel mobility ecosystem. The policy is important for energy security, import reduction, farmer income, cleaner mobility and India’s long-term climate commitments.  Key Highlights
  • Union Minister for Petroleum and Natural Gas Shri Hardeep Singh Puri launched E85 fuel at an IndianOil retail outlet in New Delhi on the occasion of World Environment Day 2026 (June 5, 2026).
  • E85 is a high: ethanol blended fuel comprising 80–85 per cent ethanol and 14–19 per cent petrol, speci昀椀cally designed for use in 昀氀ex-fuel vehicles.
  • The initiative aims to facilitate the adoption of Flex- Fuel Vehicles (FFVs), which are capable of operating on ethanol blends from E20 to E100, without restricting consumers to a single blend.
  • Ethanol blending has increased from 1.53 per cent in 2014 to 20 per cent as of June 5, 2026, with the target achieved 昀椀ve years ahead of schedule.  Bene昀椀ts of E85:
  • The first achievement was the underwater unfurling of the Indian Tricolour. Guinness World Records records this achievement under the title “Largest flag unfurled underwater.”
  • The second achievement followed with the creation of the “Tallest human stack underwater” at Swaraj Dweep. First International Conference on Transitioning Away from Fossil Fuels The First Conference on Transitioning Away from Fossil Fuels was an important step in global climat e governance because it focused specifically on how countries can reduce their dependence on coal, oil and gas in a just, orderly and equitable manner. The background to the conference lies in the COP28 Global Stocktake decision.  Guiding Principles
  • The Conference was guided by three key principles:  Participation by those ready to move forward.  Implementation as the guiding star, based on the shared scientific certainty.  Focus on solutions that meaningfully support the transition rather than delay it. India’s 1st Satellite-Tagged Ganges Soft-shell Turtle India’s first satellite-tagged Ganges soft-shell turtle, an endangered species, was released in the 1,302 sq. km Kaziranga National Park and Tiger Reserve in Assam on May 15, 2026.  Key Highlights

The Ganges soft & Related Developments

  • The Ganges soft: shell turtle ( Nilssonia gangetica ), a Schedule I animal under the Wildlife Protection Act of 1972, can be differentiated from other riverine turtles by its distinct arrowhead-shaped markings on the top of the head.
  • Ganges softshell turtle (Nilssonia gangetica) can be differentiated from other riverine turtles in having distinct arrow head shaped markings on top of head. India Ranks Third Globally in Renewable Energy Installed Capacity India ranks third in renewable electricity install ed capacity in IRENA ’s end-2025 international comparison, ahead of Brazil and behind China and the United States. The 昀椀gures below incorporate the July 2026 IRENA revision, the latest veri昀椀ed MNRE capacity snapshot and CEA ’s later generation data, checked on 18 October 2026.

Together, wind and solar generated & Related Developments

  • Together, wind and solar generated a calculated 281.456 BU, equivalent to 15.23% of the Executive Summary aggregate including Bhutan imports.

  • Installed capacity and electricity generation measure different things. Renewable capacity is part of non- fossil capacity, which also includes nuclear power. Reaching a 50% capacity share therefore does not mean that non-fossil sources generate 50% of annual electricity. COP 15 of the Convention on the Conservation of Migratory Species Migratory species are animals that move across countries, continents, oceans, rivers and skies for food, breeding, climate suitability or survival. Their protection cannot be done by one country alone because their routes cut across political boundaries. This is why the Convention on the Conservation of Migratory Species of Wild Animals (CMS) , also known as the Bonn Convention, is important for global biodiversity governance.
  • The 15 th Meeting of the Conference of the Parties to CMS (CMS COP15) was held in Campo Grande, Brazil, from March 23 to March 29, 2026, under the theme “Connecting Nature to Sustain Life.”  Major Outcome
  • The biggest outcome of COP15 was the decision to provide new or upgraded treaty protection to 40 species, subspecies and populations of birds, aquatic wildlife and terrestrial animals.
  • These were added to or upgraded within CMS Appendices I and II. With this, the Convention now covers over 1,200 unique species.  Brazil’s Role and Next COP
  • With the conclusion of COP15, Brazil holds the CMS COP Presidency for the next three years . The focus during this period will be on implementing the decisions taken in Campo Grande.
  • The next CMS COP , COP16, will be held in Bonn, Germany, in 2029 . It will coincide with the 50th anniversary of CMS , making it symbolically important because Bonn is the city where the treaty was signed in 1979. Project Him Sarovar Project Him Sarovar is a new water-security initiative of the Ladakh Administration. It has been designed for Ladakh’s cold desert conditions, where water is a lifeline for agriculture, settlements and livelihoods. The project focuses on scientific snow harvesting , conservation of snowmelt and glacial melt , and creation of small water bodies so that seasonal water, which usually flows away, can be stored and used during lean periods.  Key Highlights
  • Project Him Sarovar was launched on April 10, 2026 by Vinai Kumar Saxena, LG of Ladakh.
  • Burundi, Turkmenistan, and Togo, which ranked among the top 30 most polluted countries in 2024, are absent from this year’s report due to a lack of available data.  Global PM2.5 Trends and WHO Guideline Performance

Congo 50.2 India’s National Repository for Deep-Sea Fauna India has taken an important step in marine biodiversity conservation by designating the “Bhavasagara” Referral Centre at the Centre for Marine Living Resources & Ecology (CMLRE), Kochi, Kerala, as the National Repository for Deep-Sea Fauna on March 24, 2026. The designation was made by the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC), while CMLRE functions under the Ministry of Earth Sciences (MoES).  About Bhavasagara Referral Centre
  • The Bhavasagara Referral Centre is a specialised national facility dedicated to deep-sea biodiversity. It acts as a repository for deep-sea faunal collections from India’s Exclusive Economic Zone (EEZ).
  • Bhavasagara maintains over 3560 taxonomically identified and geo-referenced voucher specimens.
  • The repository will also support capacity building in deep-sea taxonomy. State of India’s Environment (SOE) 2026 Report The State of India’s Environment 2026 report was released by the Centre for Science and Environment (CSE) and Down to Earth Magazine on February 25, 2026 at the Anil Agarwal Dialogue 2026 in Nimli near Alwar Rajasthan. The annual SOE series began in 2014, though CSE’s longer environmental reporting tradition goes back to the 1982 Citizen’s First Report.  Key Highlights
  • The report states that 2025 recorded the highest rise in the frequency and impact of extreme weather events in India during the last four years.
  • These events included heatwaves, cold waves, heavy rainfall, and 昀氀oods.
  • From January 1 to November 30, 2025 , extreme weather events occurred on 99% of the days in India, causing 4,419 deaths and affecting at least 17.41 million hectares of crop area.
  • The IRENA Youth Forum, held on the sidelines of the Assembly, brought together young leaders from more than 60 countries to emphasise the role of youth innovation, digital skills and inclusive policy in shaping sustainable energy futures.
